Write me the line, the closed-line reference and the feed and the date of the seal, and write me nothing about the subscriber, because there is nothing to write, and then I want the racks logged back to where Otieno can return them by the channel without a question, and I want you off this by the end of the day."

Ferran looked at her a moment longer than he had looked at her in three days of work, and she watched him decide not to ask the thing he had begun to feel, the thing that did not fit a billing fraud, which was that a subscriber covering a stolen-franking exposure did not seal a closed line three days after a quiet reconciliation reader began walking the hub's batch posting toward it, that the timing of the seal was the timing of a thing that had been watched. He was good. He had felt it. He set it down, the way a contracted man who wanted to be contracted again set down the thing the client did not want him to have felt, and he drew a clean sheet and began to write the line.

Kaviel went back down the room to the dim end and stood at the racks where the daylight did not reach, and she put her hand flat on the rail of the nearest cradle, the worn wood Ferran had cut and joined in the first days, and she stood there in the second lamp's reach while the room behind her came back by degrees to its working sound, the stylus moving again at the next rack, Otieno lifting his volume, Asha's page turning at the near end, the building running on through the second hour of the morning the way it always ran while in this room five people sat at the edge of a thing that had just reached back and shut a door in their faces. She had built the room to move faster and the speed had cost her the wake she knew it would cost, and the wake had been read, and the reading had been answered before her own hand had finished reaching, and there was no part of that she had not weighed, except the part that no weighing prepared a person for, which was the flatness of it, the way it had come as a posting and not a blow, a lawful seal in a clean hand on a page, a door closed by a subscriber whose name she could not read and did not, this morning, need to read, because she knew whose office sat at the top of a closed line that ran up the spinward route toward the bureau seats, and the source had told it to her mother in a private residence outside the complex eleven days ago, and her mother had told her, and she carried it now where she carried everything she could not write.

She came back up the room.

The relay display Ferran had built ran on a frame at the near end of the table, a clean board where the trace stood mapped in three points and the lines between them, the forwarding office, the transit hub, and the closed line running up toward the third point that should by the day's end have carried a name and now carried a seal instead. Ferran had set the seal onto it before she reached the table, a small struck mark over the third point in the convention he used, the convention that meant a record reading no further, and the three points sat on the board with the road between them drawn and the end of the road closed, the trace gone dead on the display a hand's reach short of the office it had been climbing toward all week.

Kaviel watched it a moment.

"He knows we're looking."

Asha, beside her: "Then we move faster."


---

# Chapter 17 — The Protected Individual Returns

The witness facility did not announce itself. It sat below the administrative level of the Lioness compound, down a stair that the watch logged but did not guard, behind a door faced in the same gray panel as the storerooms to either side of it, and a person who did not already know what lay behind it would have read it as one more cupboard in a building full of cupboards. That was the design. Kaviel had walked the approach the day before from three directions, the way she walked every approach, and had satisfied herself that nothing in the route told a watcher where the route went.

The room itself had no window. It had been cleaned that morning, and the cleaning had left its own evidence, the faint chemical sweetness of the scrub the compound used on rooms reserved for this work, the smell that always sat at the back of Kaviel's throat and that she had stopped trying to name years ago. A table, a jug of water and two covered cups, two chairs set out and the others left stacked against the wall. A single light panel flush in the ceiling, even and shadowless. Nothing on the walls, nothing on the table but the water. She had brought no file in, because a file told a witness what you already had and taught her where the gaps were, and a witness who learned the gaps began to fill them, and a witness who filled gaps was no longer a witness.

Meret stood inside the door. She had been there since before Kaviel arrived, and she would be there until the witness had gone, and in the hours between she would say nothing and ask nothing, because that was the work Kaviel had asked of her and Meret did the work she was asked and not the work she imagined. She was in the room so that the room was secure, so that nothing came through the door that Kaviel had not let through, and she stood with her hands loose and her weight even and her eyes on the door, a presence the witness would feel and would not have to look at.

"They are bringing her up," Meret said. It was the second thing she had said since Kaviel came in. The first had been the room's condition, given in three words, which was how Meret gave conditions.

"Slowly," Kaviel said. "I want her settled before I am in front of her."

Meret put her knuckle to the bead at her collar and passed it on, two words, and they waited.

Kaviel had read the intake the way she read everything, once for the facts and once for the seams, and the seams in this intake were the part that had kept her awake. The witness had a name now. She had been a woman on a bench at Sabra Crossing with a document case held in both hands, a protected individual onward routed to a seventh gate and filed closed, and the file Kaviel had filed that night had been clean and defensible and silent on the one thing that mattered, which was that the woman Kaviel moved had come up out of Sennar on a feeder that posted to no board, under a name no one upstream had passed to Kaviel, by a route that the advance brief had gotten wrong in two of its three details. The file did not say the brief had been bad, because Kaviel had not been able to prove it, and a thing she could not prove did not go into a file. She had carried it instead, the woman's face and the bad brief and the man at the document desk with the courier's satchel, into the place she kept things she could not yet do anything with.

The door opened. An operative brought the witness in and withdrew, the way the operative at Meridian had withdrawn, a body in the doorway for the length of time it took and then gone, the door easing shut behind on its own weight.

Kaviel stood. She did not come around the table. A table between them was a thing the witness could keep, a width of it that was hers, and Kaviel left it to her.

Naila Osei was older than the memory by four years and otherwise the woman from the bench. Middling height, a record-office stillness in the way she held herself, hands that wanted an object and found none and folded instead. She had the look of a person told she was safe enough times that she had stopped believing the telling did anything, who came into a windowless room and read the door at her back the way a person reads a thing they have learned to read. She looked at Meret once, took the measure of her, and looked away. She registered the chairs left stacked against the wall, the two set out and no third for an interrogator. A records officer of the peripheral grade missed very little; the work had shaped her the way Kaviel's work had shaped Kaviel.

"Sit, if you would," Kaviel said. "There is water. The room is ours for as long as we need it and no longer than that."

Naila Osei sat. She poured herself water without being asked and did not drink it, set it where she could reach it, a record officer's instinct to have the thing in front of her placed before she needed it. "I know your face," she said. Her voice was low and even, a voice given nothing to carry. "I have spent four years not letting myself know it, and now they bring me to a room and there it is, and I find I knew it the whole time. You sat down beside me at Sabra and told me about a re-route that was overbooked, and you put a voucher on the bench, and you said seventh gate, and you walked away and did not look back."

"I did."

"I have wondered since whether you were real."

"I was the movement," Kaviel said. "I am something else now, and the something else is why you are here, and I am going to be plain with you, because you would catch me if I were not and it would cost us both the hour." She sat, finally, across the width of the table the woman had kept. "You asked, through the channel that kept you, to give what you have. No one sent for you. I want that said first, because what you give me you give freely, and that will matter later in a way I will tell you about before you leave."

"I know what I am here to give." A record officer's flatness, the flatness of a person stating the contents of a folio. "I worked the spur registry at the Meridian feeder out of Jurisdiction Seven for eleven months before they moved me. I was a reconciliation clerk. I entered the carry-forward attestations into the standing record. I am here to tell you that the attestations I entered were false, and that I knew it, and that I entered them anyway, until the day I understood what I was inside, and then I went to the channel and asked to be taken out."

Kaviel let that sit without reaching for it. It was a great deal, given fast and whole, and a witness who gave a great deal fast at the opening was a witness either telling the truth or performing it, and the difference was the thing the next hour was for.

"Tell it in order," Kaviel said. "From the work. Not from the day you understood. Tell me the work first, the way it ran on a day that was nothing, and let the day you understood arrive where it arrived. I will not be able to use the understanding if I have not first seen the floor it stood on."

Naila Osei told it the way Kaviel had hoped she would, in the dry registry voice of a person describing a process she had performed a thousand times, and the dryness was the thing that made it land. The couriers came in at the half-hour through the south gate with the attestation satchels, chopped at the shed window, and the schedules went up to the registry where she sat, and she keyed each carry-forward entry against the prior authority it cited. Where the authority checked she confirmed it into the standing record. Where it did not check she was to enter it regardless, on a separate instruction that came down with the schedule and that she was to burn.

"Who handed you the instruction to enter the ones that did not check," Kaviel said.

"The schedule carried it. A slip folded into the schedule, in a hand that was not the registrar's hand. I was to read it, enter against it, and burn it in the registry stove before the schedule went to file." She turned the water cup a quarter and did not lift it. "I burned a great many slips."

"And the courier."

Something moved in the woman's face, the first thing in it that the registry voice did not cover. "The same man, most quarters. Even features, a build you would not describe. He left the satchel at the shed window and did not wait for the chop, and a courier who does not wait for his chop is carrying paper that is not his to answer for. The shed clerk called him Beye. I never spoke to him. The schedule he left was thicker by a third than the spur's own traffic could ever have warranted, and I keyed it, and I burned the slip."

Kaviel kept her face still over the name and the satchel and the third again, because all three had come to her once already from another hand in another cold room, and a thing that arrived twice by two roads that had never touched was a thing that began to be evidence. She gave nothing, because a witness who saw recognition began to aim for it, and she let the silence open.

"You said there was a day you understood," Kaviel said. "Tell me the day."

Naila Osei was quiet for a moment, the quiet of a person walking back to a room she did not want to stand in again, and then she went in. "A black transit came down the freight spur after the day shed had closed. There is no facility chop on a transit that comes in after close; it enters the gate counter as nothing more than axles. A man came down from it. Heavy through the shoulders, deliberate, the kind of slow a man can afford who has never had to hurry. There was an aide a half-step behind him carrying nothing, and a man whose aide carries nothing is a man whose hands are kept empty on purpose." She had not lifted the cup. "He did not go to the shed. He went to the registrar's office, which was dark, and the registrar opened it for him and lit the lamp behind the blind, and they were in there the better part of an hour, and the aide stood at the door the whole time and watched the yard."

"Where were you," Kaviel said.

"At my window. I had stayed late to finish a schedule, which the registrar did not know, because the registry sits above the office and a person at its window after dark is not seen from the office door." The flatness held, and under it now there was a thing the flatness was working to hold. "I saw his face when the lamp came up. I had seen it before. The Bureau sends down a circular every year on the standardization of the attestation forms, and the circular carries a plate of the senior table at the New Terra office, the men who sit the session. His face is on that plate. He sits near the convening chair. I had keyed forms standardized at that table for eleven months, and the man whose table standardized them came down a freight spur in the dark to sit an hour in my registrar's office and leave no chop, no entry any ledger I kept could hold, and I understood, sitting at the window in the cold, what I had been entering and whose weight had been behind it."

The room was very quiet. Meret had not moved at the door. The light panel hummed in the register Kaviel felt behind her eyes more than heard, and the scrubbed sweetness of the cleaned room sat at the back of her throat, and across the table the woman who had keyed a fraud for eleven months and gone to the channel and asked to be taken out sat with her hands folded over a cup of water she would not drink, looking at the middle distance of a registry window four years and many jurisdictions away.

Kaviel did not reach for the thing she had just been given. She had it. She had read Silva's field log out of Jurisdiction Seven until she could have set it down from memory, the black transit and the heavy man, the aide who watched the yard, the dark office and the lamp behind the blind. Silva had stood in the lower yard with a folio case and watched the outside of an hour that this woman had watched from the inside, from a registry window above the same office, and the two accounts had locked together across four years and a great distance the way the face and the frame had locked together in the Meridian room, and the locking was the thing the investigation had needed and had lost when the third relay went dead.

She did not say so. She let none of it cross the table.

"The registrar," Kaviel said instead. "After the man left. What did the registrar do."

"He burned something at the canteen stove before first light. Not the registry stove where the slips went, not the terrace incinerator where the public burning is logged. The canteen stove, in the dark, before the shift came on. A man who burns at his own stove and not where it is logged is burning a thing he does not want anyone to know was ever paper." She turned the cup again. "Two days after, the aide was still on the spur. The transit was gone and the man was gone and the aide stayed, alone, at the canteen at midday with no transit in the yard. A man like that does not leave his aide on a freight spur for company. He leaves him because the spur has become part of the work and he wants a steady hand on it. That was when I went to the channel."

Kaviel sat with it. Everything the woman had given her fit the gap in the case the way the pieces Rashid Beye had given her had fit the gap above Mokoena, and the fitting was the thing that made it dangerous, because a thing that fit too well was the first thing a hostile reader reached for. She read it for the seam the way she had read Zola's columns for the place they were wrong, looking past whether the woman was right and toward where she could be torn at, and she found the tear, and the tear was not in the testimony.

The tear was in how Kaviel had her.

She turned it over while the woman sat, the cold of the lower level coming up through the floor into the soles of her boots. The testimony was clean and real, and it confirmed Silva's log down to the lamp behind the blind, and it filled the place the dead relay had left, because a clerk who had keyed the entries and burned the slips and seen the man come down in the dark was the human end of the route the trace had lost when the third relay was cut. With this the case was whole. The records, the correction pass Asha had found, Zola's full list of the three commissions, Beye's shape of the hand, Silva's field log, and now a witness who had sat inside the hour and seen the face. Whole. And whole was the new problem, because the moment Kaviel laid this testimony into the case in a form anyone could read, she laid beside it the question of how she had the witness at all, and the answer was a movement filed four years ago at Sabra Crossing on a brief bad in two of its three points, a thread Kaviel had pulled out of the air and protected on inference she had never been able to prove. The witness's value rested on her safety, her safety rested on a movement that did not appear in any record as what it had been, and to use the testimony with its provenance attached was to unwind the protection that made the witness possible.

"I am going to tell you the thing I said would matter later," Kaviel said. "You came to us freely, and you gave this freely, and that is a truth I cannot put on a page in the form it took in this room. If I write down where it came from, I write down a chain that ends at a bench at Sabra Crossing four years ago, and that chain is the thing that has kept you alive, and the moment it is on a page it has stopped keeping anyone alive and started doing the opposite. Do you follow me."

"I keyed records for eleven months," Naila Osei said. "I know what a chain on a page does."

"Then you know what I am going to do, and I am going to say it out loud so that you can hold me to it." Kaviel kept her voice level and gave the woman her eyes. "I am going to take what you have told me. The transit after close, the man who came down, the dark office and the aide at the door, the thing burned at the canteen stove, the aide left on the spur. All of it. And I am going to set it down in a form that stands on its own, that points where it points without a single line in it that runs back to you. The substance is the evidence. The road I took to reach you is not, and it is not going on any page. You came to us freely and freely is how you will leave."

Naila Osei looked at her for a long moment, the records-officer flatness over something the flatness was holding, and then she nodded, once, the nod of a person who has had a process explained correctly and has nothing to add to it.

"There is one more thing, and then I will not keep you. The man who came down. You will not name him in this room. You have his face from a Bureau plate and a seat near a convening chair, and that is enough for me to know that you saw what I needed you to have seen, and it is more than enough to be the death of you the moment it is written beside your name. So I will not ask you who he was. I am going to ask you only the plate you saw it on. The standardization circular. Could you find it again. The document, the year of it, the session it carried, so that the plate itself does the saying and you never do."

"I keyed against that circular for eleven months." Naila Osei's mouth moved, almost. "I could find it in the dark."

"Then that is the form it will take." Kaviel rose. "A clerk who keyed standardized forms, citing the standardization circular she keyed them against, naming the document and the year and the session. The plate is public. The circular is filed in every bureau office in the Outer Prefectures. A reader who pulls it sees the senior table and counts the seats and reaches his own conclusion, and no line in what I write will have told him where to count, and no line in it will run to you." She came around the table now, the first time she had crossed it, and she did it slowly, so the crossing was not a thing the woman had to brace against. "You will give the circular reference to Meret on your way out. The year, the session, the seat from the convening chair. Nothing else. She will not write your name and you will not say the man's. Do you understand the shape of it."

"I understand the shape of it," Naila Osei said. "I have spent four years understanding shapes I was not meant to see. This one I am glad to."

"Then we are finished, and you did a thing tonight that a great many people who key records and burn slips do not do, and I am not going to tell you it was brave, because you would hear the telling as a payment and I am not paying you for it." Kaviel held the door of her own attention open one last moment. "You will be taken back the way you came. The route is mine. No one on it knows what this room was for, and no one ever will."

Naila Osei stood. She finished, finally, the water she had not touched, drinking it down in the practical way of a person who does not waste a poured cup, and set the empty thing on the table squared to the edge, a record officer to the last. She looked at Kaviel a moment longer, the way the woman on the bench at Sabra had not been able to look at her, openly, taking the measure of the face she had wondered for four years whether she had imagined.

"You did not look back, at Sabra," she said. "At the time I thought it was that you did not care, that I was a parcel and the parcel was delivered. Later I understood it was the opposite. Looking back was the one thing that would have fixed us together in that hall for anyone watching, and you had already decided I was worth more to you unwatched." She picked up nothing, because she had brought nothing. "I have wanted four years to thank the woman who did not look back. I find now that I cannot, because thanking you is looking back, and you would not want it."

"No," Kaviel said. "I would not."

Meret opened the door. Naila Osei crossed to it, and at the threshold she stopped beside Meret and gave her the circular reference in a low even voice, the year and the session and the count from the convening chair, three things and nothing more, and Meret took them without writing them down, holding them the way Meret held things, until she was sure. Then the witness went out into the gray corridor and the operative who waited there, and Meret closed the door behind her, easing it to on its own weight so that the closing was a sound and not a slam.

Kaviel stood in the cleaned room with the hum of the light panel behind her eyes and the chemical sweetness at the back of her throat, and she did not move to the table, and she did not write. The case was whole on her desk two floors up, every part of it but this, and this was the part that finished it and could not be allowed to show how it had come. She would build the form for it tonight: the clerk, the circular, the public plate, the count of seats, a road a reader could walk that did not pass through any door she had opened. She had done it before, and she would do it now. She crossed to the table and squared the witness's empty cup beside her own, and she pulled out the chair, and she sat down in front of the testimony she had not yet written a word of, and she looked at the empty surface of the table a long moment before she reached for anything.


---

# Chapter 18 — Eleanor's Decision

The room had not changed, and that was the first thing Eleanor read when she came into it, the way she read everything, against the version of it she carried in her. The two chairs sat where they had sat. The low table held the tray and the two cups. The window looked out on the interior garden that neither woman had ever walked, and the path through it had gone another season unwalked since the last time, the unpruned hedge a little heavier against the glass, a gardener's tool still leaning where it had been left to rust. The light was different. The first meeting had been an evening meeting, the cold coming up off the stone with the dark behind it. This was the gray of a morning that had not decided to be warm, an even light without sun in it, and it laid no bar across the marble and gave the repair lines in the floor no edge, so that the old breaks showed only as a faint discoloring under the polish, the seams of a thing mended long ago by hands that had wanted it to look unmended.

She came in without the cane taking weight, holding it the way she held it, and she sat in the chair that had been hers before either of them was born to the room, and she set her hands folded over the head of the cane and waited for the Empress to pour.

Amira poured. She lifted the pot herself, because the absence of servants was the meaning of the room, and the steam came off the cups and broke against the cold of the walls and was gone before either of them touched the porcelain. The smell of it reached Eleanor faint and bitter and herbal, the same leaf they had drunk and not drunk the last time, and she took the cup when Amira set it an inch nearer her, wrapped both hands around it and did not drink, and let the small warmth last the minute it would last in a room this cold.

"You asked for the room," Amira said. "You have not asked for the room since the spring."

"No."

"The last time you asked for it, you were buying time you would not name. You bought two weeks. You brought me the report whole on the thirteenth day, a day under, and I have not forgotten that either."

"You flinched at none of it," Eleanor said. "I told you most rulers cannot do that. You did it. I have weighed the doing of it ever since, and it has changed what I am willing to bring into this room."

Amira drank, the cup raised and lowered with nothing on her face, and Eleanor watched the absence of expression and filed it the way she filed all such absences, as a kind of information about a person who had learned, from her, that the giving of nothing was itself a discipline. The first exchange had been made and answered, and both of them knew it for one exchange and not two. Amira had told Eleanor that she remembered the cost of the last trust she had extended. Eleanor had told Amira that the extending of it had earned her something she was about to spend.

"I have the whole of it now," Eleanor said. "Not the shape. The body. The list, and the mechanism that kept the list invisible, and the reach of the office that issued the protection. My people have it documented to the standard the throne would require if the throne were ready to require it. The scope is larger than I brought you in the spring. A thing built to seem ordinary is built wide, because width is what lets each piece of it pass for routine. This one runs across the Outer Prefectures and into the jurisdictions that touch them. Three Thrones' worth of administrative ground answers, in the end, to one chair."

Amira set the cup down. The small sound of porcelain on wood was the loudest thing the room had made. Outside, past the garden no one watched, a bell in the administrative wing marked an hour, the morning hour this time and not the evening one, a higher and busier sound with the day's work moving under it, and it faded and left the stillness in the room heavier than the noise had been.

"A chair," Amira said.

"A chair, and the man in it, and I know the man." Eleanor turned the cup a quarter in her hands. "I have known him the better part of forty years. I took his measure in rooms quieter than this one and found it sound, and I was wrong, and the being wrong is mine to carry. You do not need his name this morning. You will have it when you need it, and the having of it will be a weapon, and I will not put a weapon in the room before I have told you what I mean to do with the hand that would wield it."

Amira's eyes came up at that and stayed. She had a way of going still that was not the stillness of waiting; it was the stillness of a mind that had heard a thing arrive ahead of its meaning and was clearing room for the meaning to land. The garden window let the gray light fall across the side of her face and gave the cheekbone no shadow, and she let the quiet run, because she had learned from the woman across the table that the quiet after a sentence was where the sentence finished itself.

"You have decided something," Amira said. "You came in deciding it, the way you came in deciding the two weeks. You let me open the conversation so the thing you want will arrive as the answer to a question instead of a demand into silence. You taught me the maneuver, and I use it on petitioners who do not know they are being used by it, and it is strange to sit on the near side of it and recognize the hand."

"Then I will not maneuver," Eleanor said. "It is too important to waste the hour on technique, and you are too able to make it cost more than it earns. I am going to ask you for a thing, and ask it plainly, and the plainness is the only assurance I can offer that I am not handling you."

"Ask."

"Three days. Before any formal action. Before the name moves out of this room, before a warrant is drawn, before a single office of the establishment learns that the throne has seen what I have seen. Three days in which the throne does nothing and instructs no one and signs nothing. After the three days, the timing is yours, and the name is yours, and the apparatus I have built is yours to point wherever you point it. I will not ask for an hour past the three days. I have never asked you for time I wasted. I am not asking now."

Amira did not answer at once. She poured into her own cup, though the first was not finished, the way a person pours to fill the space where a decision is being made, and the fresh liquid steamed and the steam died against the cold and the bitter smell of it hung a moment and thinned. She set the pot down and looked at the cup and not at Eleanor, because to look at Eleanor while deciding was to hand Eleanor the decision early, and she had learned that from Eleanor too.

"In the spring," Amira said, "you told me what the two weeks were for. You told me there was a person whose continuance depended on the matter being handled in a way that did not announce how you had learned of it. You named the cost without naming the person. You bought the time to protect a source. I extended the trust because the request had a shape I could see the back of, even where I could not see the face."

"Yes."

"This request has no back to it." Amira turned the cup a half on the table, the porcelain dragging faint on the wood. "You are asking me to do nothing for three days, and you are not telling me what you will do in them. You are not telling me what the three days buy. You are not telling me whether you mean to act inside them or only to wait. You are asking for the time and withholding the use of the time, and that is a different request than the spring, and we both know it is, and you have come in this morning knowing I would know."

"I have."

"Then tell me why I should grant it."

Eleanor was quiet a moment. The cold of the room had reached her hands fully now, and the cup gave back nothing, and she set it down on the tray with both hands so that it would not rattle, and folded her hands again over the cane.

"Because you have granted it before," she said, "to a harder version of me, in this room, for a use the throne was never told. Your grandmother granted me time once whose purpose I never gave her and never will. She did not like granting it. She granted it because the alternative to trusting the house was governing without the house, and she had measured both, and the house was the colder comfort and the surer one. I watched her decide it. You are sitting this morning where she sat. I am asking you to do the thing she did, and to like it as little as she liked it, and to do it anyway, because the arithmetic has not changed in two hundred years and will not change for our convenience."

"My grandmother granted you time to protect a source," Amira said. "That is the work the compact is for. I understood her the first time you sat in that chair. You are asking me for time to do a thing you will not describe, and I cannot tell, from where I sit, whether it serves the throne or serves your house or serves only you, and you are asking me to grant it without resolving which."

"I am."

"Eleanor."

The name, plainly used, was a pressure laid at a point, the way it had been the last time, and Eleanor felt the point and did not move from it. Amira did not use the given name often. In the throne room she said De Grosvenor, or she said nothing and let a look carry it. Here, with no scribe to read familiarity into it, the name was an instrument, and the woman wielding it had learned the wielding from the woman she aimed it at.

"You are afraid," Amira said, "that if you tell me what the three days are for, I will forbid it. You have measured the odds that I would, and found them high enough that you would rather ask for the time blind than ask for it whole and be refused. You have decided to do something I would not permit if I knew it, and you have come to buy the room to do it in. That is the request. Say whether it is the request."

Eleanor looked at her across the low table and the two cups going cold, and the gray light lay on both of them and gave neither a shadow, and the seams in the floor showed faint under the polish where the old breaks had been mended to look whole.

"It is near enough that I will not insult you by correcting the margin," she said. "Yes. There is a thing I mean to do that you would forbid, and you would be right by your own measure to forbid it, and the forbidding would cost the throne more than the doing, and the judging is mine because it is the work my house exists to do. I am asking you to lend me three days of doing nothing while I take onto my house a risk the throne should not be seen to take and cannot be made to wear. The compact was drafted by people who did not trust each other and needed each other regardless. This is the use it was drafted for, the ugliest and the rarest, the reason the terms were written so they could be honored without ever being comfortable."

Amira rose then. She did it without haste, smoothing the front of her robe with one hand, and crossed to the window she had been ignoring, and stood before the glass with her hands joined behind her at the small of her back in the posture she took on the dais when a petition ran long and she wished to weigh it without a hundred faces reading her. The pane had fogged faintly where the cold of the glass met the warmer air, and her breath added to it, and through the blur the dead path and the rusting tool and the heavy hedge lost their edges and became a gray field with shapes in it.

She stood a long time. The bell in the administrative wing marked another hour, the day deepening under it, the ordinary traffic of the establishment moving through corridors that did not know what was being decided in a room without a scribe. Eleanor did not fill the silence. She had taught the Empress that a ruler who fills her own silences has surrendered them.

"If I grant the three days," Amira said to the glass, "and the thing you do inside them goes wrong, I will have granted the time, and the granting is the throne's act even where the doing is yours. You are asking me to put my name to a blank page and trust the house to write nothing on it I would be unable to stand behind."

"No," Eleanor said. "I am asking you to put your name to nothing at all. There is no page. The three days exist only as an absence, an action the throne declines to take, and an absence cannot be read back to you as authorization, because you will have authorized nothing, signed nothing, instructed no one. If the thing I do goes wrong, it is a thing the head of the De Grosvenor house did on her own standing, and the throne's defense is the truth, that it knew nothing and was governing in good faith on the information it had. I have built it so the absence protects you. That is the half of this you have not yet seen. The three days are a thing I give you, dressed as a thing I ask."

Amira turned from the window. The fogged glass had not cleared, and behind her the gray field of the garden held its blurred shapes, and the light came in flat and laid no shadow on the floor between them.

"You have built it so that I am protected whether I grant the time or refuse it," she said. "If I refuse, the throne moves in the open and you are not a factor in it. If I grant, I do nothing, and the doing of nothing cannot be turned against me, and whatever you do inside the three days is yours to wear alone. You have built a request I cannot lose by answering, which means you have built it so that the only loser available is you."

"I have weighed who can afford the loss," Eleanor said. "I am old. The house outlives its heads; that is the point of a house. The throne does not outlive a misstep taken in the open at the wrong hour. I am the cheaper thing to spend, in any accounting that runs longer than a life, and I have run my accounting longer than my life since before you were crowned."

Amira came back to the chair and sat. She had not meant to sit again; standing had been the closing posture, the signal that the meeting neared its end, and to sit again was to reopen what she had begun to close, and Eleanor marked the reopening and gave no sign of marking it.

"There is a version of this," Amira said, "in which the three days are for your house, and the protection you describe is the wrapping you put on it so I would take it. I cannot rule it out from where I sit. The man at the top of the thing has sat at tables with you for forty years, and the shape of your request is also the shape it would take if you meant to reach him for a reason of your own."

Eleanor did not look away from it. "You would be a poor ruler if you did not say it. If my house were the danger, I would not spend my standing to buy time I could have taken without asking. I would have brought you the list incomplete and steered you past the large part of it while you congratulated yourself on the small. That I have brought you the whole, and built the three days so the cost falls on me and the protection on you, is the behavior of a house that is not betraying you. It is not proof. There is no proof. It is the behavior, offered in the place where proof does not exist, and you will weigh it as that or you will not grant the time."

The two of them sat with the cold cups between them and the gray light on the mended floor, and the bell in the wing did not ring, and the garden held its blurred shapes behind the unwiped fog on the glass. Amira looked at her hands a while, and then at the woman across the table, and the looking was the decision arriving and being read in the same moment, because there was no longer a use in concealing it from each other.

"Three days," Amira said. "From this hour. The throne does nothing, instructs no one, signs nothing. I will hold the morning's work as though this conversation had not occurred, and I will be, in the matter, a ruler governing in good faith on the information she has, which will be true, because I will refuse the rest of the information until the three days have run. When they have run, you bring me the name, and you bring it to this room, and there is no scribe, and after that the timing is mine again and you do not ask me for an hour past it."

"After that the timing is yours," Eleanor said.

"I am not granting this because I trust the thing you will do. I do not know the thing you will do. I am granting it because in the whole of my reign your house has not failed the throne in a way I could prove, and because the request is built so that the only person it can ruin is the person asking for it, and a house bent on betrayal does not build the trap so that it springs on itself." Amira set both hands flat on the low table, the gesture deliberate, the way a person sets down a weight she has chosen to carry rather than drop. "And because my grandmother granted you the same and the throne survived it. I did not understand her then. I understand her this morning. I dislike understanding her. I am doing it regardless."

"That is the whole of what the compact ever asked of either of us," Eleanor said. "To do the thing we dislike, regardless, because the disliking is cheaper across the years and dearer in the hour. We have not improved on the people who drafted the terms. We have only kept the house they built, where the roof leaks at the windows they wanted, and learned to set the buckets without complaint."

Amira rose again, and this time the rising held, and the room came back to its proper order, the Empress on her feet and the head of the serving house seated, the given names set down and the titles taken up. She moved toward the door that led to her quarters, and at it she paused, not turning fully, her profile lit cold and flat from the garden window she still had not looked at, the fog of her own breath gone from the glass behind her.

"The thing you mean to do," she said. "When you bring me the name. I will want to know whether you did it because the compact required it, or because you chose it and called it the compact."

"You will not like the answer," Eleanor said.

"I rarely do."

Eleanor rose then, slowly, the cane taking weight for the first time since she had entered, and she set the untouched cup back on the tray with both hands so that it would not rattle, and she inclined her head the precise depth that the head of the De Grosvenor house inclined it to the Trinitarian Throne, and no further, and the gray light lay even on the mended floor between them, and the door to the Empress's quarters closed without sound.

---

She told Kaviel in a corridor.

It was not the office, where the single lamp burned past the eighth hour, and it was not the investigation room, where the team now worked the days at full capacity. It was a corridor in the administrative wing, a length of cold stone between two doors that led somewhere else, the kind of place a person crosses without staying, and Eleanor stopped in the middle of it where the light from a high window fell in a flat band across the floor and did not warm it, and Kaviel stopped because her mother had stopped.

Eleanor did not turn to face her fully. She stood with the cane set and her weight even on it and looked at the band of light on the stone, and she said it in the voice she used for instructions, the voice that asked nothing of the room.

"I am going to the man myself," she said. "Alone. Before any formal action. The Empress has granted three days. They begin this morning. On the third day I bring his name to her and the timing returns to the throne. Between now and then I will see him, and you will not be present, and you will not arrange a presence, and you will hold the room and the list and the team exactly as they are and move nothing. If anyone asks what the house is doing in these three days, the house is doing what it always does, which is waiting."

Kaviel said nothing. She had been trained from before she could write that the thing her mother did in the minute after she set a weight down was a thing done alone even when another was in the room, and she gave the minute now, and held it, and marked the holding and meant never to mention it.

She did not ask why. She knew her mother would not have built the three days, would not have spent the standing in the reception room, would not have stood in a cold corridor to say it in the instruction voice, if there had been a version of the thing that ran through the apparatus and stayed safe. She knew the man's name; she had been against the wall in the dead woman's house, in the chair the light did not reach, and had heard the old magister say it without weight. She knew that a person who carries a name like that into a room alone, before the throne has moved and before the apparatus can stand behind her, does it so that whatever happens in the room falls on one person and not on the house and not on the throne. She knew her mother had built the three days the way she built everything, so that the cost would land where Eleanor had decided it should, which was on Eleanor. And she knew there was no instrument she could offer that her mother had not already weighed and set aside, because an instrument would only move the cost off the place Eleanor had chosen for it, and to argue the choice was to ask the old woman to be someone other than the one who had raised her to recognize exactly this and not look away.

"Three days," Kaviel said.

"Three days."

"And nothing moves until you bring her the name."

"Nothing moves."

Eleanor turned her head then, the smallest degree, enough to bring her daughter into the edge of her looking and no further, and whatever she found in Kaviel's face she did not search, and Kaviel gave her the same nothing she had given the magister in the dead woman's house, the correct nothing, the only form of agreement that did not insult the gravity of the thing. Eleanor inclined her head a fraction, a smaller depth than she gave the throne, one that had no name and was kept only for the girl, and she turned and crossed the band of cold light and went out through the far door, the cane taking its even weight on the stone, and the sound of it faded by degrees and was gone.

Kaviel stood alone in the corridor. The high window let the flat light down across the stone and the band of it lay where her mother had been standing, and from somewhere below in the administrative wing came the smell of the day's first meal carried up through the shared vents, bread and something cooked too long, the smell of a building that did not know, and a clerk's footsteps passed in the corridor beyond the far door and did not slow, and the cold came up through the floor and found her where she stood without moving.

She had read documents her life long for the part the writer did not think to guard, and she read this the same way, against her will and without trying, and she saw what her mother meant to do and why it had to be done alone and why the doing of it placed the old woman at the end of a thing from which the house could not reach her, and she stood in the cold band of light in the empty corridor and understood the whole of it, and wished, with everything she had been trained never to show and showed now to no one because there was no one to show it to, that she did not.


---

# Interlude 4 — Historical Note on the De Grosvenor Compact

I set down here what the record permits me to establish about the agreement under which the De Grosvenor house has served the Trinitarian Throne, because the decision Eleanor De Grosvenor took in the months this account concerns cannot be weighed without it. A reader who does not know the terms of the compact will read her choice as recklessness. A reader who knows them will read it differently, and I believe the second reading is the truer one. I will keep to what the documents support and mark where they do not.

The compact predates both the Empress Amira and Eleanor by a wide margin. Its founding instrument survives in the standing archive in two copies, one held under the Throne's seal and one under the seal of the house, and the two agree in every clause, which is itself a thing worth noting, since instruments of that age usually drift apart in the copying. It was formalized under the administrative reforms that followed the reign of Lucius Luceron II, the Soldier Emperor, who left behind a Throne that governed by process where his predecessors had governed by presence. The compact belongs to that work. It was drafted by clerks, not by heroes, and its language is the flat language of an establishment instrument. That flatness has preserved it. Three imperial generations have passed under it without amending a word.

The terms are short. The house places its intelligence and administrative apparatus at the disposal of the reigning sovereign. The sovereign, in turn, grants the house a latitude no other instrument in the imperial body grants to anyone: the head of the house may act on the sovereign's behalf without prior authorization where seeking that authorization would defeat the action, and the sovereign agrees in advance to be bound by what is done in good faith under that latitude, whether or not the sovereign would have approved it had there been time to ask. I have read the clause perhaps two hundred times. It is the most dangerous sentence in the imperial archive, and it was written by a clerk who understood exactly what he was setting down. The compact does not ask the sovereign to trust the house. It requires the sovereign to be bound by trust already spent, after the fact, when the cost has already landed.

Such an instrument should have failed within a generation. It did not, and the reason it did not is the part of the record I most want the reader to carry forward. The archive shows three prior occasions on which the latitude was exercised at its full reach, each under a different sovereign and a different head of the house, and each preserved in enough detail to study.

The first came under an early reign I will not date precisely, the founding instrument being then less than thirty years old. An earlier matriarch of the house suppressed a report the sovereign had ordered released, held it through a season of pressure that cost her standing she never recovered, and was vindicated only when the source she had protected by holding it proved to be the single thread by which a succession crisis was unknotted. The sovereign had not authorized the holding. The sovereign was bound by it. The record notes, dryly, that the matriarch and the sovereign did not speak again for two years and that the apparatus functioned without interruption throughout.

The second I can date with confidence to the middle reign of the three, the household accounts of that period being intact. The head of the house in that generation moved imperial funds across a jurisdictional boundary, without the sovereign's knowledge, to settle a debt the sovereign did not know the Throne had incurred, and absorbed the irregularity into the house's own books rather than enter it where it could be traced to the sovereign. The accounts carry the loss to this day, unrecovered. The sovereign learned of it eleven years later, from a man in my own posting, reading the same kind of pages I read now.

The third is recent enough that men still living remember its public face, though almost none of them know its private one. The head of the house in that instance withheld the name of a compromised official from a sovereign who had every right to demand it, withheld it for the length of time required to be certain, and bore alone the risk that the certainty would never come. It did come. The official fell. The reign held.

In each of the three the same shape appears, and the shape is the point. One party extends trust the other has not formally earned and cannot formally compel, and does so under a clause that converts that trust into an obligation the sovereign cannot afterward refuse. The compact does not survive because the parties are loyal. Loyalty wears thin under three generations. It survives because the instrument was built to spend trust ahead of proof and to make the spending binding, so that the question is never whether the sovereign will agree, the question is only whether the head of the house will dare to act knowing the sovereign was never asked.

This brings me to Eleanor De Grosvenor and to the decision she took when she had learned the full reach of the matter recorded in these pages and learned, with it, the name at the top of it, a name she had known across decades. She did not bring that name to the Empress and ask leave to act. She asked instead for three days and gave no reason, and the Empress granted them, and the granting was itself an act under the compact, trust spent forward against a proof not yet shown. What Eleanor intended to do in those three days she told no one, and I have not reconstructed it from anything she said, because she said nothing I can cite. I have reconstructed it from the instrument. By the terms of the compact she was about to exercise the latitude at its full reach for the fourth time in the agreement's history, alone, against an official whose authority spanned three Thrones' worth of administrative ground, before any formal action could shield her, knowing that the moment she moved he would know he had been found.

The wax on the founding copy of the compact has gone the color of old tea, and the seal of the house pressed into it has lost the fine lines of its device, so that I must turn the page to the angled light from the high window to read the matrix at all. It was made by a clerk who knew where an empire is held upright at its worst hour. The weight does not rest with the sovereign, and it does not rest with the house. It rests on the willingness of one of them to act first and be judged after. What follows in this record is that clause being exercised as it was always built to be exercised, by the one person then alive who understood it well enough to pay what it asks.


---

# Chapter 19 — What Eleanor Said

The room had been built for nobody to own. Eleanor knew the kind before she was through the door, because she had ordered the choosing of it herself and given her people the qualities to find: a chamber that belonged to no office, sat on no one's corridor, owed its furniture to no house and its history to no reign. It was a meeting room in a commercial registry tower on the lower terraces of New Terra, the sort of place where freight factors and bonded agents signed paper neither of them trusted the other to honor, leased by the hour through a third name and emptied of its clerks for the afternoon. The walls were a pale stone that took no echo. A long table of some pale resined wood ran down the center, scarred at the corners where strangers had set their cases down hard, and the chairs along it were identical and faced one another across a width measured so that no one at the table could lean and reach another without standing first. There were no windows on the courtyard side. The single window faced out over the freight terraces, and through it came the flat continuous light of a working district and, under everything, the low vibration of the loading engines that never stopped, a sound felt in the table more than heard.

She had come early. She sat with her back to the inner wall and her face to the door and the cane standing against the table's edge where her hand could find it, and she did not pour the water that had been set out, two flasks and four glasses on a tray in the middle, because to have drunk before he came would have been to have begun without him, and she had built this hour to begin only when both of them were in it. The vibration of the engines came up through her hands where they rested folded on the wood. The light did not change. She waited the way she had waited in rooms for sixty years, without the waiting showing as anything a watcher could have named, and a watcher would have seen an old woman in a plain coat sitting very still in a leased room, which was the whole of what she wished to be seen.

He came at the hour she had named, alone, which told her the first thing.

Lord Marshal Tewodros Marek was a tall man who had grown heavier with his years and carried the weight as a kind of settledness, the way some men in high office learn to occupy more room as a courtesy to the people who must find them in it. He wore the dark establishment coat without its marshal's collar, which was its own message, that he had come as a man and not as the office, that he wished the afternoon read that way. His face was broad and dark and deeply lined at the eyes, a face that had spent decades arranging itself into the patient attention senior administrators wore in committee, and it was arranged that way now. He shut the door behind him with the flat of his hand, without looking to see that it had latched, and he crossed to the chair opposite hers and stood a moment behind it with both hands on its back.

"Eleanor," he said.

"Tewodros."

"You chose a hard place to find. I had a man drive past it twice."

"I chose it so that neither of us would have to wonder whose floor we were standing on," she said. "Sit, if you mean to. I have an hour I would rather not spend watching you decide whether the chair is mine."

He smiled at that, a small real smile that touched the lined eyes, and he drew the chair and sat, and the two of them faced each other across the measured width of the pale table with the water untouched between them and the engines vibrating in the wood. He set his hands on the table, broad and ringless, and looked at her for a while in the way of a man taking the measure of a thing he had thought he already knew, and she let him look, because she was doing the same and saw no reason to pretend either of them was doing otherwise.

"You poured nothing," he said.

"I do not drink with a man until I know what we are to each other across the table," she said. "We will know within the hour. There is water if you want it. I am old and I do not, in the afternoons."

"How long has it been," he said, "since you and I sat at a table with no one's name on it."

"You will be doing the arithmetic to find the answer flattering," she said. "I will save you the trouble. It has been long enough that the last time, the woman on the throne was the grandmother, and you were two ranks below the chair you sit in now, and I had already begun to be old. I did not come to count years with you. You know I did not. Let us not spend the hour furnishing it."

The smile went, without offense in its going. He folded his hands and the lines at his eyes settled into the committee patience, and she understood that the first exchange was finished and that he had let her end the warmth because he had wanted to see whether she would, and that her ending it had told him as much as his offering it had told her. They had taught each other a great deal across forty years. Some of it she was about to use against him, and he knew that she was, and the knowing sat in the room between them as plainly as the water.

"You have been into my house," he said.

"I have been into the establishment's house," she said. "Yours by occupancy, not by deed. The distinction will matter to the people who come after me, and it does not matter to you and me this afternoon, so set it aside. Yes. I have been into it. I have the carry-forward entries and the commissions they claim and the commissions that did not sit. I have the correction pass and the category of authorization it ran under, the category your office governs and no one beneath it can open. I have the man in Adwa and the courier who fed him and the relay that went dark when we reached for it. I have the reach of it, three Thrones of ground answering to one chair, and I have the chair, and I have had the chair for some weeks now, and I came to you before I did anything else with it, which is the only reason you are sitting in a leased room with an old woman instead of reading about yourself in a warrant."

He took that without his face moving, which she respected. A lesser man would have reached for the water to have something to do with his hands. He left them folded.

"You came to me first," he said. "Before the throne."

"I did not say before the throne."

She watched the small recalculation move behind the patient face and not surface, and she gave it the time it needed. He was not a stupid man and she had never once thought him one, and the worst thing she could do in this hour was let him believe she thought it now. He had reached the marshalcy of the Bureau of Establishments over thirty years by being right about people more often than he was wrong, by reading a room and a roster and a balance the way she read a held report, and the thing she had come to offer him depended on his being exactly that able, because a fool would refuse it for the wrong reasons and she could do nothing with a fool's refusal.

"Then the throne knows," he said.

"The throne knows there is a thing," she said. "The throne does not yet know your name. The throne has granted me three days in which to do one thing, and this is the thing, and you are most of the second day of it. On the third day I bring your name into a room with the Empress in it, or I bring her something else. Which of the two I carry up those stairs depends on the next part of this hour, and not on anything that happens after it, so I would have you attend to it closely."

"You are offering me something."

"I am offering you one thing," she said. "Once. I will say it plainly, because you are too able to be handled and I would insult us both by trying. You will resign the marshalcy on a stated ill health, effective at a date I will give you, near enough that it reads as sudden and far enough that it reads as a man's own choice. You will sign, before you go, a reconciliation of the carry-forward record that unwinds the false entries under cover of the same backlog provision that hid them, so that the establishment corrects itself from the inside and the throne is spared the cost of being seen to cut into its own administration in the open. You will give me, in writing and to me alone, the names above the courier and below yourself, the working parts of the thing, the people who cannot be left in the establishment whatever becomes of you. And you will live. Quietly, off the rolls, in whatever house you have put aside against your age, with the pension the office carries and no charge ever drawn, because a charge drawn against you is a trial, and a trial is the throne cutting into itself in front of three Thrones of watching administrators, and the throne would rather not, and so would I, and so, I think, would you."

The engines vibrated up through the table. The light over the freight terraces did not change. He sat with his folded hands and his patient face and looked at her across the measured width, and he was quiet a long time, and she did not fill the quiet, because she had taught half the court that the quiet after an offer belongs to the one who received it, and she had taught herself the same lesson long before there was a court to teach.

"And in exchange," he said at last, "I give you the house. The names, the record, the unwinding. I hand you the whole of it and trust that a charge is never drawn, on the strength of an old woman's word in a room with no scribe in it, while I sit in a house off the rolls waiting to learn whether the throne agrees with you about what it would rather not do."

"Yes."

"You have not changed," he said, and there was something in it that was almost fondness and was not quite. "You build the room so that the only safe move is the one you have chosen, and you call it an offer. I have watched you do it to men who never knew it had been done to them. I know it is being done to me, and I will tell you what I see when I look at the room you have built, and you will hear me out the way you would have me hear you, because we are too old to do each other the discourtesy of pretending the other cannot count."

"Count, then."

"You have the record," he said. "I do not doubt it; you would not be here if you did not, and you have never bluffed me, in forty years, which is itself a thing I have relied on. But the record is the surface. The record is the appointments, and the appointments are the part of the thing that was always going to be found if anyone looked the way your people look, and the part of the thing that can be unwound the way you describe, because it was built to be unwound, because it was built to be the part that takes the fall if a fall is ever needed. You have found the structure that was meant to be found. You are very good, Eleanor, and you have found exactly the thing that a careful man would leave for you to find."

She did not move. Inside, behind the stillness she had worn into the bones of her face, a cold small certainty arrived and seated itself, and she held her hands folded on the wood and let no part of the arrival reach the surface, because the worst thing she could do now was let him see that he had told her something she had not known.

"Go on," she said.

"I will not give you the names," he said. "Not because I cannot. Because the offer assumes the appointments are the thing, and the appointments were never the thing. They were the visible house. They were what we let stand in the daylight so that the rest could run in the floor beneath it. You have costed your three days against the surface. You will spend them unwinding the surface and bringing the Empress a clean correction and a quiet resignation, and you will believe you have closed it, and the floor will go on running, because the floor does not touch the appointments at any point a record search would cross. You will have to find it the long way, the way you found this, and that is years, and I do not have to give you a single name to buy those years, because the structure that funds the thing was never written where your reconciliation trace can reach it."

"A second mechanism," she said. She said it flatly, as a clerk reads back a figure to confirm it, and she watched him hear that she had not known and had it now.

"I have said more than I came meaning to," he said, and for the first time he reached for the water, and poured a glass, and the small ordinary sound of it filled the room, and the chill of the flask had beaded the glass and he turned it once in his fingers before he drank. "You always did get more out of a conversation than the other man meant to spend. I will not name it. I have told you it is there because telling you it is there is the answer to your offer, and the answer is no. You cannot give me my life in exchange for a house you have already half taken, when the part of the house that matters is one I can keep by saying nothing and no more, and outlast you in the keeping, because I am younger than you by eleven years and the floor does not need me at the marshalcy to go on running. You have built the room well. You built it for the wrong house."

She let it stand. The thing he had given her sat in her like a stone swallowed, heavy and clean and certain, and she turned it once where it lay and understood its dimensions and set about, in the same minute, the work of carrying it out of the room without his seeing the weight of it. He had refused. The refusal was the door she had been authorized to walk through if it opened, and it had opened, and now there was only the leaving and what she would carry through it.

"You have read me correctly," she said, "about what I can do this afternoon. You have read me incorrectly about what I will do tomorrow. You believe that because the second thing is hidden the long way, I will be forced to spend years on it, and that the throne will not move against an establishment it cannot yet prove a crime in, and that an old woman of eighty does not have the years. All of that would be true if the throne and I were bound to act through the channels you have spent thirty years learning to govern. We are not, after today. You have just told me the floor exists. I did not know it existed an hour ago. I will know its shape inside a week, because a thing that funds an apparatus for years leaves a different kind of trace than a thing that hides for a season, and I have a daughter who reads floors for a living, and you have just told her where to put her feet."

Something crossed his face then, the first thing in the hour that he had not chosen to show, and it was not fear, because he was not a man who feared in rooms. It was the recognition of a man who had said one sentence more than the structure could afford, and had said it to the one person in three Thrones who could spend it. He set the glass down.

"You came to give me a way out," he said.

"I came to give you the only one there was," she said. "You have refused it. I want it understood between us, in this room with no scribe, that the offer was real, that I would have honored every line of it, that a man who took it would be alive and off the rolls and unpursued tonight. I do not make offers I will not keep. You know that I do not. You have refused a real thing, with your eyes open, because you have read the odds and decided that silence and eleven years are the better wager. It is a calculation, and it is not a stupid one, and I have made it the heart of my report that it was not stupid, because a throne that mistakes you for a fool will lose to you, and I do not intend the throne to lose to you."

"You have already named me to the throne, then. In all but the word."

"I will name you tomorrow," she said. "Today I have done you the courtesy of the hour, and you have done me the discourtesy of the floor, and we are, I think, square."

She put both hands to the head of the cane and rose, and the rising took its weight on the cane the way it did now, and she did not hurry it, because to hurry would have been to let him see that she wished to be out of the room and alone with what she carried. He rose when she rose, as the manners of forty years required, and he stood behind his chair with his hands on its back as he had stood when he came in.

"They know it is you," he said. "I will tell you that for nothing, because you gave me the hour. The structure knows there is an investigator and the structure knows the investigator wears your house. It knew before you walked in here. Your name and your daughter's name are on a page in an office you have not found, and the page was written some days ago. You have not been moving in the dark you think you have been moving in. I tell you because you told me a true thing about my odds and I will pay you back the one true thing I can afford, that the cost of this has already been entered against your house, and was entered before you set this room. Whatever you do tomorrow, you do it already named, and so does she."

She received it standing, with her hand on the cane and her face turned to him and giving back the same nothing she had worn for the whole of the hour, and inside she set the second stone beside the first, the named house and the named daughter, and she weighed it once and found it a cost she had priced before she came and would pay without revising the figure.

"Then we have each given the other one true thing this afternoon," she said. "It is more than most men manage in a day, and we managed it across a table built so neither of us could reach the other. Goodbye, Tewodros. I do not expect we will sit again."

"No," he said. "I do not expect it either." And then, as she turned for the door, in a voice that had dropped the committee out of it and held, for one sentence, something nearer to what they had been to each other in the years before this one, "It was a good offer, Eleanor. I want you to know that I heard it as one. I am not refusing it because I think little of it. I am refusing it because I can count."

"I know," she said, with her back to him and her hand on the door. "It is the only reason I respected it enough to make."

She went out, and she did not look back, and the door latched behind her on its own weight.

The corridor outside was a low service hall that ran to the freight lifts, lit by the same flat continuous light, smelling faintly of lubricant and cold metal and the burnt-grain reek that came up off the loading engines through the floor grates. She walked it slowly, the cane setting its even sound on the composite floor, and at the turn toward the public transit corridor a man stepped back against the wall to let her pass, a courier in the gray coat of no house, a soft round face she had cause to know from a manifest and a controlled encounter her daughter had run in the spring, Rashid Beye standing with a sealed case held flat against his chest, waiting on the wrong floor of the wrong tower for a meeting he would now never be sent to. His eyes found her and went wide and then went flat with the discipline of a man who had been trained to be no one, and he inclined his head a careful degree, as one stranger yields a corridor to an old woman, and she gave him nothing at all, not the smallest sign that the face meant anything, and passed him, and heard behind her the small wet sound of a man swallowing as he understood whose afternoon he had walked into and what it meant that she had not bothered to acknowledge him.

The transit corridor was wider and colder, with the public lifts at its end and the noise of the district coming up the shaft, and benches set against the wall for the agents and factors who waited there between their meetings. She stopped at the first of them. She did not sit. She set the cane against the bench and stood with her weight on it and reached into the inner pocket of the plain coat and took out the small notebook she had carried for thirty years, black, soft at the corners, the kind a person uses who does not trust a thing to a system she does not own. She uncapped the short pencil that lived in its spine. She held the notebook open against the flat of her hand, and the flat light came down even on the page, and the engines vibrated up through the floor and through the cane and into her, and she stood a moment with the pencil not yet touching the paper.

Then she wrote three words.

She read them once. She closed the notebook over the pencil and pressed the soft cover flat with her thumb and returned it to the inner pocket, and she took up the cane, and the lift at the end of the corridor opened its doors on a car already half full of people who did not know her, and she crossed the cold floor toward it at her own pace and did not look back down the hall toward the room or the courier or the door that had latched on its own weight, and the cane set its even sound on the composite, and she walked.


---

# Chapter 20 — Kaviel Tells Amira

The room had no window, and that was why it had been chosen. It sat below the level of the Empress's working quarters, reached by a stair that doubled on itself twice so that a person descending lost the count of the turns, and the air in it was the air a sealed space keeps, drier than the corridors above, with the faint mineral smell of cut stone that never warms. The light came from two fixtures recessed into the ceiling, even and shadowless, the kind of light that does not change with the hour because there is no hour for it to answer to. A table ran the length of the room, broad enough to lay documents flat without overlapping them, and along one wall a row of cabinets stood locked. There was nothing in the room that a scribe would sit at. There was no third chair.

Kaviel had come down first and set the room, because she had asked for the room and the asking carried the obligation. She laid the folders along the table in the order she meant to take them, each squared to the edge, and she did not sit while she waited, because the work she had brought did not permit the posture of waiting. The single lamp she kept in her own office, the one she cracked a window beside to move the air, had no equivalent here. The air did not move. She stood at the head of the table with her hands at her sides and listened to the room give back nothing, no traffic, no bell, no footstep, the sealed quiet of a place built so that what passed inside it left no trace in the building that held it.

Amira came down alone. She came without the household that attended her above, without the two who walked the corridor a pace behind her elbow, and the absence of them was the meaning of the descent, the same meaning the small reception room carried when she lifted the pot herself. She wore the plainer of her working robes, the one without the weight at the shoulders, and she came into the room and looked once down the length of the table at the squared folders and the locked cabinets and the even light, and she understood the room before Kaviel said a word, because she had governed long enough to read a room the way Eleanor read a face.

"You set this below the records floor," Amira said.

"Two levels below it. There is no register of the room being opened. There is no register of either of us being in it." Kaviel inclined her head, the depth a senior Lioness gave the Throne, exact and no further. "The house keeps three rooms like this. This is the one furthest from the administrative wing."

"Furthest from your mother's office."

"Furthest from every office," Kaviel said, and let the correction stand without sharpening it.

Amira moved to the head of the table and did not sit, and so Kaviel did not move to sit either, and the two of them stood over the laid documents in the shadowless light. The Empress put one hand flat on the table near the first folder, not opening it, the way a person rests a hand on a thing she has not yet decided to lift.

"My household believes I am reviewing prefectural petitions," she said. "That is a thing I do alone and at irregular hours, and it is true often enough that it is true now. Begin. I will hold my questions until you have laid the whole of it. You will tell me when the whole of it is laid."

"That is how I have ordered it," Kaviel said.

She began with the surface, because the surface was where the throne had first looked and looked away, and she laid the Sennar magistrate's record open on the table and set beside it the carry-forward entry that confirmed the appointment under a prior commission's authority, and she put her finger on the two dates that could not both be true, the confirmation that preceded the session that confirmed it. She did not narrate the finding as a discovery. She gave it as a fact already established, documented, dated, traced, and she moved her hand from the Sennar record to the next folder without pausing on the satisfaction of it, because the satisfaction was months old and the work had gone far past it.

Amira looked at the two dates. She had signed off on the procedural finding in that matter, had sat through the petition and ruled on it and thought no more of it, and the record of her ruling was somewhere in the floors above this one with her own confirmation on it.
